This page explains what they are, how they differ, and how Kubernetes schedules them.\n     9\t\n    10\t| DeviceClass | Resource type | Use case |\n    11\t|---|---|---|\n    12\t| `gpu.nvidia.com` | Full GPU | Exclusive or shared access to a single physical GPU |\n    13\t| `mig.nvidia.com` | MIG slice | Hardware-isolated partition of a supported GPU |\n    14\t| `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com` | VFIO passthrough | Raw GPU access for workloads that manage the driver themselves |\n    15\t\n    16\t---\n    17\t\n    18\t## Resource types\n    19\t\n    20\t### Full GPUs\n    21\t\n    22\tA full GPU gives a container exclusive access to a single physical GPU. This is the default allocation mode and requires no additional configuration.\n    23\t\n    24\tFull GPUs support optional sharing for cases where you want to divide the GPU across multiple workloads:\n    25\t\n    26\t- **Consumable capacity:** independent `ResourceClaim` objects can share a multi-allocatable device while Kubernetes tracks the capacity consumed by each claim.\n    27\t  The NVIDIA driver requires its `ConsumableShares` feature gate and a `consumableShares` mode.\n    28\t- **Time-slicing:** containers take turns on the GPU using CUDA preemption. Requires the `TimeSlicingSettings` feature gate.\n    29\t- **MPS (Multi-Process Service):** containers run concurrently with configurable thread percentage and memory limits. Requires the `MPSSupport` feature gate.\n    30\t\n    31\tTarget DeviceClass: `gpu.nvidia.com`\n    32\t\n    33\tSee [`demo/specs/quickstart/`](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/tree/{{< param driver_release_tag >}}/demo/specs/quickstart) for configuration examples.\n    34\t\n    35\t---\n    36\t\n    37\t### MIG slices\n    38\t\n    39\tMIG (Multi-Instance GPU) partitions a MIG-capable NVIDIA data center GPU (such as A100, A30, H100, and newer) into independent hardware slices. Each slice has its own dedicated compute engines, memory bandwidth, and L2 cache — workloads are fully isolated at the hardware level.\n    40\t\n    41\tUnlike time-slicing and MPS, MIG partitioning is enforced by the GPU hardware itself. A container claiming a MIG slice cannot be affected by activity on another slice of the same physical GPU.\n    42\t\n    43\tMIG slices are identified by profiles such as `1g.5gb` or `3g.20gb`, where the first number is the fraction of GPU compute and the second is the dedicated memory allocation. The available profiles depend on the physical GPU model.\n    44\t\n    45\t#### Static and dynamic MIG\n    46\t\n    47\tMIG support has two modes:\n    48\t\n    49\t- **Static MIG:** the node administrator pre-configures MIG partitions using\n    50\t  `mig-parted` or `nvidia-smi`. The driver discovers existing partitions and\n    51\t  exposes them as allocatable devices. This is enabled by default; no feature\n    52\t  gate is required.\n    53\t- **Dynamic MIG:** the driver creates and destroys partitions automatically\n    54\t  based on workload requests. Requires the `DynamicMIG` feature gate.\n    55\t\n    56\tStatic and dynamic MIG differ only in *who* creates the partitions, not in how\n    57\tyou request them. The same `ResourceClaimTemplate` and pod manifests work in\n    58\tboth modes.\n    59\t\n    60\t#### How static MIG discovery works\n    61\t\n    62\tStatic MIG requires no feature gate and no MIG-specific driver configuration.\n    63\tThe driver always discovers pre-configured MIG partitions, so a default install\n    64\t(`resources.gpus.enabled=true`) is all that is needed on the driver side. All of\n    65\tthe setup happens on the node, before the driver starts.\n    66\t\n    67\tThe GPU kubelet plugin discovers partitions at startup and advertises each one as\n    68\ta device in the node's `ResourceSlice`:\n    69\t\n    70\t- Discovery is a point-in-time snapshot. Partitions added or changed after the\n    71\t  driver starts are not picked up until the GPU kubelet plugin restarts.\n    72\t- MIG mode enabled with no partitions strands the GPU. If a GPU has MIG mode\n    73\t  enabled but no partitions configured, the driver advertises neither the full\n    74\t  GPU nor any slices for it, and logs a warning.\n    75\t\n    76\t#### How dynamic MIG works\n    77\t\n    78\tWith dynamic MIG, the driver creates and destroys partitions automatically based\n    79\ton workload requests, so you do not pre-configure them on the node.\n    80\t\n    81\tDynamic MIG builds on the Kubernetes partitionable devices feature (KEP-4815).\n    82\tIt works in three phases:\n    83\t\n    84\t1. **Advertise every possible partition.** At startup, the GPU kubelet plugin\n    85\t   enumerates all MIG profiles and every valid placement for each profile on\n    86\t   each GPU, then advertises all of them in the node's `ResourceSlice`, even\n    87\t   though none exist on the hardware yet. Alongside the devices, it publishes\n    88\t   shared counters (one set per physical GPU) that track compute, memory, and\n    89\t   memory-slice usage. Each advertised partition declares how much of those\n    90\t   counters it would consume. This lets the scheduler allocate a partition that\n    91\t   does not yet exist, and prevents handing out two partitions that would\n    92\t   physically overlap on the same GPU.\n    93\t2. **Create the partition on demand.** After the scheduler allocates a partition\n    94\t   to a pod, the GPU kubelet plugin creates the actual MIG device when it\n    95\t   prepares the pod's resources on the node — enabling MIG mode on the GPU if\n    96\t   needed, then creating the GPU instance and compute instance for the requested\n    97\t   profile and placement.\n    98\t3. **Destroy the partition when it is no longer needed.** When the claim is\n    99\t   released, the driver tears the MIG device back down. The plugin uses a\n   100\t   node-local checkpoint as the source of truth, so it can reclaim partitions\n   101\t   left behind by a crash or partial preparation when it restarts.\n   102\t\n   103\tA request can be satisfied even when no matching partition was pre-configured —\n   104\tthe driver creates one to match.\n   105\t\n   106\tMIG slices support consumable capacity, time-slicing, and MPS sharing, using the same approaches as full GPUs.\n   107\tTime-slicing on MIG slices does not support interval configuration.\n   108\t\n   109\tTarget DeviceClass: `mig.nvidia.com`\n   110\t\n   111\tSee the [Request full GPUs guide](../guides/gpu-allocation/allocating-gpus.md) and\n   112\t[`demo/specs/quickstart/`](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/tree/{{< param driver_release_tag >}}/demo/specs/quickstart)\n   113\tfor GPU configuration examples. For MIG setup and workload examples, see the [MIG guide](../guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md).\n   114\t\n   115\t---\n   116\t\n   117\t### VFIO passthrough\n   118\t\n   119\tVFIO (Virtual Function I/O) passes a full physical GPU directly to a container, bypassing the NVIDIA driver stack in the host kernel. This gives the container raw hardware access and is intended for workloads that manage the GPU driver themselves, such as virtual machine managers or specialized research environments.\n   120\t\n   121\tVFIO passthrough has no sharing options; one container gets one GPU.\n   122\t\n   123\tTarget DeviceClass: `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com`\n   124\t\n   125\tRequires the `PassthroughSupport` feature gate (Alpha, default: false). See [Feature gates](../reference/feature-gates.md) to enable it.\n   126\t\n   127\t---\n   128\t\n   129\t## Choosing a resource type\n   130\t\n   131\t| | Full GPU | MIG slice | VFIO passthrough |\n   132\t|---|---|---|---|\n   133\t| Hardware isolation | No | Yes | Full device |\n   134\t| Sharing supported | Yes (consumable capacity, time-slicing, or MPS) | Yes (consumable capacity, time-slicing, or MPS) | No |\n   135\t| Supported hardware | All NVIDIA GPUs | MIG-capable data center GPUs (A100, A30, H100, and newer) | All NVIDIA GPUs |\n   136\t| Feature gate required | No (sharing gates optional) | No | `PassthroughSupport` (Alpha) |\n   137\t| Typical use case | ML training, general workloads | Multi-tenant inference, strict isolation | VM passthrough, specialized environments |\n   138\t\n   139\t### Add optional allocation features\n   140\t\n   141\tChoose a resource type first, and then add only the features that apply to that\n   142\tresource:\n   143\t\n   144\t- **Sharing across claims:** Kubernetes DRA consumable capacity lets independent claims share a full GPU or MIG device by memory capacity, without a capacity limit, or by a fixed share count.\n   145\t  Refer to [Consumable capacity](../guides/gpu-allocation/consumable-capacity.md).\n   146\t- **Sharing within a claim:** time-slicing or MPS can share supported full-GPU or MIG allocations among containers that reference the claim.\n   147\t  A device configuration uses one sharing strategy.\n   148\t- **Fabric topology:** Fabric Manager partitioning constrains a claim to one\n   149\t  complete NVSwitch partition.\n   150\t  The partitioning applies to full GPUs and VFIO devices, not MIG slices.\n   151\t  `PassthroughSupport` is required if you want to use VFIO devices.\n   152\t- **Operational features:** health checking and device metadata modify\n   153\t  supported workflows without creating another resource type.\n   154\t\n   155\tRefer to [Feature gate constraints](../reference/feature-gates.md#constraints)\n   156\tfor the combinations enforced at driver startup.\n   157\tFor the topology workflow, refer to\n   158\t[Fabric Manager partitioning](../guides/gpu-allocation/fabric-manager-partitioning.md).\n   159\t\n   160\t---\n   161\t\n   162\t## How scheduling works\n   163\t\n   164\tWhen a pod references a `ResourceClaim`, Kubernetes uses the following flow to allocate and inject the GPU:\n   165\t\n   166\t```mermaid\n   167\tsequenceDiagram\n   168\t    participant User\n   169\t    participant API as Kubernetes API\n   170\t    participant Scheduler\n   171\t    participant Kubelet\n   172\t    participant Plugin as GPU Kubelet Plugin\n   173\t    participant Runtime as Container Runtime\n   174\t\n   175\t    User->>API: Create Pod + ResourceClaim\n   176\t    API->>Scheduler: Schedule pod\n   177\t    Scheduler->>Plugin: Query available ResourceSlices\n   178\t    Scheduler->>API: Bind ResourceClaim to device + node\n   179\t    API->>Kubelet: Start pod on bound node\n   180\t    Kubelet->>Plugin: NodePrepareResources (claim + device + config)\n   181\t    Plugin->>Plugin: Generate CDI spec\n   182\t    Plugin->>Kubelet: Return CDI device names\n   183\t    Kubelet->>Runtime: Start container with CDI devices\n   184\t    Runtime->>User: Container running with GPU\n   185\t```\n   186\t\n   187\tThe key components in this flow:\n   188\t\n   189\t- **ResourceSlice:** the kubelet plugin publishes each node's GPU devices in a single pool (one or more ResourceSlices), advertising the devices it makes allocatable and their attributes.\n   190\t- **ResourceClaim:** the user's request for a specific type of GPU device, optionally with a configuration (GpuConfig, MigDeviceConfig, or VfioDeviceConfig) embedded as opaque parameters.\n   191\t- **CDI (Container Device Interface):** the standard used to inject devices into containers. The kubelet plugin generates a CDI spec at prepare time; the container runtime reads it to set up the device files, environment variables, and mounts.\n   192\t\n   193\t---\n   194\t\n   195\t## Publishing GPUs in ResourceSlices\n   196\t\n   197\tThe GPU kubelet plugin discovers the GPUs on each node and publishes them as `ResourceSlice` objects. One pool per node, all under the `gpu.nvidia.com` driver. The make up of a `ResourceSlice` depends on how each GPU is configured:\n   198\t\n   199\t- A GPU available as a full device is published with device `type: gpu` and you can request it through the `gpu.nvidia.com` DeviceClass.\n   200\t- A GPU with MIG mode enabled is published as its MIG slices instead of a full GPU. Each MIG slice is assigned `type: mig` and you can request it through the `mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass. With dynamic MIG, the driver also advertises every possible MIG partition plus per-GPU shared counters. See [How dynamic MIG works](#how-dynamic-mig-works) above.\n   201\t- A GPU prepared for VFIO passthrough is published with device `type: vfio` and you can request it through the `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com` DeviceClass.\n   202\t\n   203\tThe driver discovers the node's GPUs once, when the GPU kubelet plugin starts, and that inventory stays fixed while the plugin runs. It republishes `ResourceSlices` at runtime to reflect allocation, as devices are claimed and released, and, if `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck` is enabled, device health changes. The GPU kubelet plugin does not re-scan hardware. If you make a manual change to a node's GPU or MIG configuration, for example, reconfiguring MIG with `nvidia-smi`, you must restart the GPU kubelet plugin for the changes to take effect in the resource pool.\n   204\t\n   205\tThe `ResourceSlices` contain the attributes and capacity details for all the GPUs on your cluster. To review the `ResourceSlices` on your cluster, see [View available GPU resources](../guides/gpu-allocation/view-resources.md); for what each attribute and capacity field means, see [ResourceSlice device attributes](../reference/resourceslice-attributes.md).\n   206\t\n   207\t\n   208\t## Requesting a GPU\n   209\t\n   210\tResource request contains the following information:\n   211\t\n   212\t*  A `ResourceClaimTemplate` (or `ResourceClaim`) references one of the available DeviceClasses\n   213\t* Optionally, CEL selectors match the GPU attributes your workload requires\n   214\t* Optionally, an opaque driver configuration block for `GpuConfig`, `MigDeviceConfig`, or `VfioDeviceConfig` sharing and device behavior.\n   215\t\n   216\tThe scheduler uses these details to pick a matching device from the node's ResourceSlice.\n   217\t\n   218\tRefer to the [Kubernetes DRA documentation](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/dynamic-resource-allocation/) for details on the  claim and templating mechanics.\n   219\t\n   220\tFor more details on using these with the DRA, refer to the how-to guides:\n--- /Users/bmozaffari/Binaries/NVIDIA/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/dynamic-mig.md\nnl: /Users/bmozaffari/Binaries/NVIDIA/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/dynamic-mig.md: No such file or directory\n--- /Users/bmozaffari/Binaries/NVIDIA/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/site/content/docs/reference/resourceslice-attributes.md\n     1\t---\n     2\ttitle: ResourceSlice device attributes\n     3\tlinkTitle: ResourceSlice attributes\n     4\tweight: 40\n     5\tdescription: NVIDIA GPU, MIG, and VFIO device attributes and capacity published by the DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs in ResourceSlices.\n     6\t---\n     7\t\n     8\tThe DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs publishes each allocatable device — full GPUs, MIG\n     9\tslices, and VFIO passthrough devices — as an entry under `spec.devices` in a\n    10\tnode's `ResourceSlice`. This page is a reference for the NVIDIA-specific\n    11\tattributes and capacity on those entries: what each field means and the exact\n    12\tnames you reference in CEL selectors.\n    13\t\n    14\tFor the generic `ResourceSlice` type schema — every field on `ResourceSlice`,\n    15\t`Device`, `DeviceAttribute`, `DeviceCapacity`, and related types — see the\n    16\t[Kubernetes API reference for ResourceSlice v1](https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/kubernetes-api/resource/resource-slice-v1/).\n    17\tFor how the driver publishes slices, see\n    18\t[Publishing GPUs in ResourceSlices](../concepts/gpu-allocation.md#publishing-gpus-in-resourceslices).\n    19\tTo inspect the ResourceSlices on your own cluster, see\n    20\t[View available GPU resources](../guides/gpu-allocation/view-resources.md).\n    21\t\n    22\t## Device types\n    23\t\n    24\tThe DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs publishes all devices under a single driver,\n    25\t`gpu.nvidia.com`, with one pool per node. The NVIDIA-specific `type` attribute on\n    26\teach device identifies the kind of device, and the built-in DeviceClasses select\n    27\ton it:\n    28\t\n    29\t| `type` | DeviceClass | Device |\n    30\t|---|---|---|\n    31\t| `gpu` | `gpu.nvidia.com` | Full physical GPU |\n    32\t| `mig` | `mig.nvidia.com` | MIG slice |\n    33\t| `vfio` | `vfio.gpu.nvidia.com` | VFIO passthrough device |\n    34\t\n    35\tThe sections below show a representative `spec.devices[]` entry for each type.\n    36\t`kubectl` prints map keys alphabetically (so `type` and `uuid` appear last), the\n    37\t`#` comments are annotations rather than part of the real output, and the values\n    38\tare illustrative — confirm them on your own cluster.\n    39\t\n    40\t## Full GPU (type: gpu)\n    41\t\n    42\t<!-- gpuModuleID with ID, cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/deviceinfo.go:236 -->\n    43\t\n    44\t```yaml\n    45\t- attributes:\n    46\t    addressingMode:\n    47\t      string: HMM                   # memory addressing mode, when available\n    48\t    architecture:\n    49\t      string: Ampere                # GPU architecture\n    50\t    brand:\n    51\t      string: Nvidia                # GPU brand\n    52\t    cudaComputeCapability:\n    53\t      version: 8.0.0                # CUDA compute capability\n    54\t    cudaDriverVersion:\n    55\t      version: 13.0.0              # CUDA driver version\n    56\t    driverVersion:\n    57\t      version: 580.126.20          # NVIDIA driver version\n    58\t    gpuModuleID:\n    59\t      int: 1                        # Fabric Manager GPU module ID, when enabled\n    60\t    partition2:\n    61\t      int: 4                        # ID of a reported size-2 FM partition\n    62\t    productName:\n    63\t      string: NVIDIA A100-PCIE-40GB # product name reported by NVML\n    64\t    resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID:\n    65\t      string: 0000:65:00.0          # PCI bus address in BDF notation, when available\n    66\t    resource.kubernetes.io/pcieRoot:\n    67\t      string: pci0000:64            # PCIe root complex identifier, when available\n    68\t    resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n    69\t      int: 0                         # NUMA node, when available\n    70\t    type:\n    71\t      string: gpu                   # device kind: gpu, mig, or vfio\n    72\t    uuid:\n    73\t      string: GPU-2fa81118-5a5f-aa66-7660-471eed407181\n    74\t  capacity:\n    75\t    memory:\n    76\t      value: 40Gi                   # total GPU memory\n    77\t    # On MIG-capable GPUs with partition metadata, additional capacities\n    78\t    # (multiprocessors, copyEngines, decoders, encoders, jpegEngines, ofaEngines)\n    79\t    # may also appear here.\n    80\t  name: gpu-0\n    81\t```\n    82\t\n    83\t## MIG slice (type: mig)\n    84\t\n    85\t```yaml\n    86\t- attributes:\n    87\t    addressingMode:\n    88\t      string: HMM\n    89\t    architecture:\n    90\t      string: Ampere                # inherited from the parent GPU\n    91\t    brand:\n    92\t      string: Nvidia\n    93\t    cudaComputeCapability:\n    94\t      version: 8.0.0\n    95\t    cudaDriverVersion:\n    96\t      version: 13.0.0\n    97\t    driverVersion:\n    98\t      version: 580.126.20\n    99\t    parentUUID:\n   100\t      string: GPU-2fa81118-5a5f-aa66-7660-471eed407181  # physical GPU hosting this instance\n   101\t    productName:\n   102\t      string: NVIDIA A100-PCIE-40GB # inherited from the parent GPU\n   103\t    profile:\n   104\t      string: 1g.5gb                # MIG profile, e.g. 1g.5gb or 3g.20gb\n   105\t    resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID:\n   106\t      string: 0000:65:00.0\n   107\t    resource.kubernetes.io/pcieRoot:\n   108\t      string: pci0000:64\n   109\t    resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n   110\t      int: 0                         # inherited from the parent GPU, when available\n   111\t    type:\n   112\t      string: mig                   # device kind\n   113\t    uuid:\n   114\t      string: MIG-1a2b3c4d-5e6f-7a8b-9c0d-1e2f3a4b5c6d\n   115\t  capacity:\n   116\t    copyEngines:\n   117\t      value: \"1\"                    # dedicated copy engines\n   118\t    decoders:\n   119\t      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated video decoders\n   120\t    encoders:\n   121\t      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated video encoders\n   122\t    jpegEngines:\n   123\t      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated JPEG engines\n   124\t    memory:\n   125\t      value: 4864Mi                 # dedicated slice memory (usable amount, below the \"5gb\" label)\n   126\t    multiprocessors:\n   127\t      value: \"14\"                   # streaming multiprocessors dedicated to the slice\n   128\t    ofaEngines:\n   129\t      value: \"0\"                    # dedicated optical-flow accelerators\n   130\t  name: gpu-0-mig-1g.5gb-0\n   131\t```\n   132\t\n   133\t## VFIO passthrough (type: vfio)\n   134\t\n   135\t```yaml\n   136\t- attributes:\n   137\t    deviceID:\n   138\t      string: \"0x20b0\"              # PCI device ID\n   139\t    gpuModuleID:\n   140\t      int: 1                         # Fabric Manager GPU module ID, when enabled\n   141\t    iommuFDEnabled:\n   142\t      bool: true                    # whether the IOMMUFD backend is enabled\n   143\t    partition1:\n   144\t      int: 8                         # ID of the size-1 Fabric Manager partition\n   145\t    partition2:\n   146\t      int: 4                         # ID of the size-2 Fabric Manager partition\n   147\t    partition4:\n   148\t      int: 2                         # ID of the size-4 Fabric Manager partition\n   149\t    partition8:\n   150\t      int: 1                         # ID of the size-8 Fabric Manager partition\n   151\t    productName:\n   152\t      string: NVIDIA A100-PCIE-40GB # product name reported by NVML\n   153\t    resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n   154\t      int: 0                         # NUMA node, when available\n   155\t    resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID:\n   156\t      string: 0000:65:00.0          # PCI bus address in BDF notation, when available\n   157\t    resource.kubernetes.io/pcieRoot:\n   158\t      string: pci0000:64            # PCIe root complex identifier, when available\n   159\t    type:\n   160\t      string: vfio                  # device kind\n   161\t    uuid:\n   162\t      string: GPU-2fa81118-5a5f-aa66-7660-471eed407181\n   163\t    vendorID:\n   164\t      string: \"0x10de\"             # PCI vendor ID (0x10de = NVIDIA)\n   165\t  capacity:\n   166\t    addressableMemory:\n   167\t      value: 40Gi                   # addressable device memory\n   168\t  name: gpu-vfio-0\n   169\t```\n   170\t\n   171\t## NUMA locality\n   172\t\n   173\tThe GPU kubelet plugin publishes the standard\n   174\t`resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode` attribute for full GPUs, MIG devices, and\n   175\tVFIO devices when the PCI NUMA node is available and non-negative.\n   176\t\n   177\tBy default, the attribute uses the scalar `int` form shown in the examples.\n   178\tWhen you enable both the driver and Kubernetes `DRAListTypeAttributes` feature\n   179\tgates, the ResourceSlice serialization changes to a one-element `ints` list:\n   180\t\n   181\t```yaml\n   182\tresource.kubernetes.io/numaNode:\n   183\t  ints:\n   184\t  - 0\n   185\t```\n   186\t\n   187\tThis representation change matters when inspecting or parsing ResourceSlices,\n   188\tbut it does not change the attribute name that you specify in ResourceClaims,\n   189\tlike the following example:\n   190\t\n   191\t```yaml\n   192\tapiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\n   193\tkind: ResourceClaimTemplate\n   194\tmetadata:\n   195\t  name: same-numa-gpus\n   196\tspec:\n   197\t  spec:\n   198\t    devices:\n   199\t      requests:\n   200\t      - name: gpus\n   201\t        exactly:\n   202\t          deviceClassName: gpu.nvidia.com\n   203\t          allocationMode: ExactCount\n   204\t          count: 2\n   205\t      constraints:\n   206\t      - requests:\n   207\t        - gpus\n   208\t        matchAttribute: resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode\n   209\t```\n   210\t\n   211\tThis constraint requires all devices selected for `gpus` to have the same\n   212\tpublished NUMA value. It uses the same\n   213\t`resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode` spelling for both the scalar `int` and\n   214\tone-element `ints` representations.\n   215\t\n   216\t### Troubleshooting: verify the NUMA value\n   217\t\n   218\tList each GPU device with its node, PCI bus ID, and published NUMA value:\n   219\t\n   220\t```bash\n   221\tkubectl get resourceslices -o json | jq -r '\n   222\t  (\n   223\t    [\"NODE\", \"DEVICE\", \"PCI_BUS_ID\", \"PUBLISHED_NUMA\"],\n   224\t    (\n   225\t      .items[]\n   226\t      | select(.spec.driver == \"gpu.nvidia.com\")\n   227\t      | .spec.nodeName as $node\n   228\t      | .spec.devices[]\n   229\t      | .basic.attributes as $attrs\n   230\t      | select($attrs[\"resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID\"] != null)\n   231\t      | ($attrs[\"resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode\"] // {}) as $numa\n   232\t      | [\n   233\t          $node,\n   234\t          .name,\n   235\t          $attrs[\"resource.kubernetes.io/pciBusID\"].string,\n   236\t          (\n   237\t            if $numa.int != null then ($numa.int | tostring)\n   238\t            elif $numa.ints != null then ($numa.ints | join(\",\"))\n   239\t            else \"omitted\"\n   240\t            end\n--- /Users/bmozaffari/Binaries/NVIDIA/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/site/content/docs/reference/feature-gates.md\n     1\t---\n     2\ttitle: Feature gates\n     3\tlinkTitle: Feature gates\n     4\tweight: 20\n     5\tdescription: Available feature gates, their stages, defaults, and constraints.\n     6\t---\n     7\t\n     8\tFeature gates control experimental and beta functionality in the DRA Driver for NVIDIA GPUs. They follow [Kubernetes feature gate conventions](https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/command-line-tools-reference/feature-gates/).\n     9\t\n    10\t## Set feature gates\n    11\t\n    12\tSet feature gates in your Helm values file:\n    13\t\n    14\t```yaml\n    15\tfeatureGates:\n    16\t  TimeSlicingSettings: true\n    17\t  MPSSupport: true\n    18\t```\n    19\t\n    20\tOr pass them at install time:\n    21\t\n    22\t```bash\n    23\thelm install dra-driver-nvidia-gpu oci://registry.k8s.io/dra-driver-nvidia/charts/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu \\\n    24\t    --version {{< param \"driver_version\" >}} \\\n    25\t    --namespace dra-driver-nvidia-gpu \\\n    26\t    --set \"featureGates.TimeSlicingSettings=true\"\n    27\t```\n    28\t\n    29\t## Available feature gates\n    30\t\n    31\tThe Kubernetes column identifies additional Kubernetes version or Kubernetes feature gate requirements beyond the baseline versions in [Prerequisites](../prerequisites.md).\n    32\t\n    33\t| Feature gate | Stage | Default | Kubernetes | Description |\n    34\t| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n    35\t| `TimeSlicingSettings` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables customization of CUDA time-slicing settings in `GpuConfig`. |\n    36\t| `MPSSupport` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables Multi-Process Service (MPS) sharing strategy in `GpuConfig` and `MigDeviceConfig`. |\n    37\t| `IMEXDaemonsWithDNSNames` | Beta | `true` | No requirements. | IMEX daemons use DNS names instead of raw IP addresses for peer communication. Required by `ComputeDomainCliques`. |\n    38\t| `PassthroughSupport` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables VFIO passthrough device allocation using `VfioDeviceConfig`. |\n    39\t| `DynamicMIG` | Alpha | `false` | v1.34 through v1.35 requires `DRAPartitionableDevices=true` on the kube-apiserver and kube-scheduler.  v1.36 and later enables `DRAPartitionableDevices` by default. See [Kubernetes feature gates](https://kubernetes.io/docs/reference/command-line-tools-reference/feature-gates/#DRAPartitionableDevices). | Enables dynamic MIG device allocation and reconfiguration. See [MIG](../guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md). |\n    40\t| `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables GPU health checking using NVML. |\n    41\t| `ComputeDomainCliques` | Beta | `true` | No requirements. | Uses `ComputeDomainClique` CRD objects to track IMEX daemon membership. Requires `IMEXDaemonsWithDNSNames`. |\n    42\t| `CrashOnNVLinkFabricErrors` | Beta | `true` | No requirements. | Causes the kubelet plugin to crash rather than fall back to non-fabric mode when NVLink fabric errors are detected. |\n    43\t| `DeviceMetadata` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables IOMMU API device exposure, such as `/dev/iommu` or `/dev/vfio/vfio`, for VFIO workloads with `VfioDeviceConfig`. Requires `PassthroughSupport`. |\n    44\t| `FabricManagerPartitioning` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Enables Fabric Manager partition discovery and lifecycle management for full GPUs and VFIO devices on supported HGX and single-node NVL systems. VFIO devices require `PassthroughSupport`. Requires Fabric Manager with `FABRIC_MODE=1`. Each full-GPU or VFIO claim on a participating node must exactly match one published partition. See [Fabric Manager partitioning](../guides/gpu-allocation/fabric-manager-partitioning.md). |\n    45\t| `HostManagedIMEXDaemon` | Alpha | `false` | No requirements. | Allows `resources.computeDomains.imex.mode=hostManaged`, where you manage the host `nvidia-imex` service instead of the DRA Driver creating per-ComputeDomain daemon DaemonSets. This gate only unlocks the mode. Driver-managed DNS daemon naming and `ComputeDomainClique` tracking are not used in this mode. See [Validate host-managed IMEX](../guides/compute-domain-workloads.md#validate-host-managed-imex). |\n    46\t| `DRAListTypeAttributes` | Alpha | `false` | v1.36 or later with `DRAListTypeAttributes=true` on the kube-apiserver and kube-scheduler. | Publishes list-valued DRA device attributes, including `resource.kubernetes.io/numaNode` as a one-element list. See [NUMA locality](resourceslice-attributes.md#numa-locality). |\n    47\t| `ConsumableShares` | Alpha | `false` | v1.34 through v1.35 requires `DRAConsumableCapacity=true` on the kube-apiserver, kube-controller-manager, kube-scheduler, and kubelet; v1.36 and later enables `DRAConsumableCapacity` by default; see [Kubernetes consumable capacity](https://kubernetes.io/docs/concepts/scheduling-eviction/dynamic-resource-allocation/#consumable-capacity). | Implements Kubernetes DRA consumable capacity for full GPUs and MIG devices by publishing them as multi-allocatable and defining their capacity request policies; also set the [`consumableShares`](helm-values.md#feature-gates) Helm value to select an accounting mode; see [Consumable capacity](../guides/gpu-allocation/consumable-capacity.md). |\n    48\t\n    49\t## Constraints\n    50\t\n    51\tThe following feature gate combinations are mutually exclusive and cannot be enabled together.\n    52\t\n    53\t| Combination | Reason |\n    54\t| --- | --- |\n    55\t| `DynamicMIG` + `PassthroughSupport` | Mutually exclusive |\n    56\t| `DynamicMIG` + `MPSSupport` | Mutually exclusive |\n    57\t| `PassthroughSupport` + `NVMLDeviceHealthCheck` | Mutually exclusive |\n    58\t\n    59\tThe feature gates below have the following dependencies:\n    60\t\n    61\t| Feature gate | Requires |\n    62\t| --- | --- |\n    63\t| `ComputeDomainCliques` | `IMEXDaemonsWithDNSNames` |\n    64\t| `DeviceMetadata` | `PassthroughSupport` |\n--- This function is tied to PassthroughSuppert and hence for now\n   307\t// guaranteed to not be exercised when DynamicMIG is enabled.\n   308\tfunc (d *PerGPUAllocatableDevices) RemoveSiblingDevices(device *AllocatableDevice) {\n   309\t\tvar pciBusID string\n   310\t\tswitch device.Type() {\n   311\t\tcase GpuDeviceType:\n   312\t\t\tpciBusID = device.Gpu.pciBusID\n   313\t\tcase VfioDeviceType:\n   314\t\t\tpciBusID = device.Vfio.PciBusID\n   315\t\tcase MigStaticDeviceType:\n   316\t\t\t// TODO: Implement once/if static MIG is supported in the context of\n   317\t\t\t// PassthroughSupport.\n   318\t\t\treturn\n   319\t\tcase MigDynamicDeviceType:\n   320\t\t\t// TODO: Implement once/if dynamic MIG is supported in the context of\n   321\t\t\t// PassthroughSupport.\n   322\t\t\treturn\n   323\t\t}\n   324\t\n   325\t\tfor _, sibling := range d.allocatablesMap[pciBusID] {\n   326\t\t\tif sibling.Type() == device.Type() {\n   327\t\t\t\tcontinue\n   328\t\t\t}\n   329\t\t\tswitch sibling.Type() {\n   330\t\t\tcase GpuDeviceType:\n   331\t\t\t\tdelete(d.allocatablesMap[pciBusID], sibling.Gpu.CanonicalName())\n   332\t\t\tcase VfioDeviceType:\n   333\t\t\t\tdelete(d.allocatablesMap[pciBusID], sibling.Vfio.CanonicalName())\n   334\t\t\tcase MigStaticDeviceType:\n   335\t\t\t\t// TODO\n   336\t\t\t\tcontinue\n   337\t\t\tcase MigDynamicDeviceType:\n   338\t\t\t\t// TODO\n   339\t\t\t\tcontinue\n   340\t\t\t}\n   341\t\t}\n   342\t}\n   343\t\n   344\t// Taints returns a copy of the device's taints to prevent data races\n   345\t// when being read concurrently by the ResourceSlice builder.\n   346\tfunc (d *AllocatableDevice) Taints() []resourceapi.DeviceTaint {\n   347\t\treturn slices.Clone(d.taints)\n   348\t}\n   349\t\n   350\t// AddOrUpdateTaint adds a new taint or updates an existing one with the same\n   351\t// key. The value and effect are always updated to the latest event received.\n   352\t// Meaning, if a device receives multiple events for the same taint dimension\n   353\t// (e.g., XID 48 followed by XID 63), the value is overwritten and only the most recent event data is retained.\n   354\t// Returns true if the taint set was modified.\n   355\tfunc (d *AllocatableDevice) AddOrUpdateTaint(taint *resourceapi.DeviceTaint) bool {\n   356\t\tfor i, existing := range d.taints {\n   357\t\t\tif existing.Key == taint.Key {\n   358\t\n   359\t\t\t\t// 1. If nothing actually changed, exit early to avoid API calls\n   360\t\t\t\tif existing.Value == taint.Value && existing.Effect == taint.Effect {\n   361\t\t\t\t\treturn false\n   362\t\t\t\t}\n   363\t\n   364\t\t\t\t// 2. Otherwise, update the fields and the timestamp\n   365\t\t\t\td.taints[i].Value = taint.Value\n   366\t\t\t\td.taints[i].Effect = taint.Effect\n   367\t\t\t\td.taints[i].TimeAdded = nil // reset timestamp for the API server\n   368\t\t\t\treturn true\n   369\t\t\t}\n   370\t\t}\n   371\t\n   372\t\t// 3. Key doesn't exist yet, append the dereferenced struct\n   373\t\td.taints = append(d.taints, *taint)\n   374\t\treturn true\n   375\t}\n   376\t\n   377\t// RemoveTaint removes the taint with the specified key. It returns true when a\n   378\t// taint was removed.\n   379\tfunc (d *AllocatableDevice) RemoveTaint(key string) (resourceapi.DeviceTaint, bool) {\n   380\t\tfor i, taint := range d.taints {\n   381\t\t\tif taint.Key == key {\n   382\t\t\t\td.taints = slices.Delete(d.taints, i, i+1)\n   383\t\t\t\treturn taint, true\n   384\t\t\t}\n   385\t\t}\n   386\t\treturn resourceapi.DeviceTaint{}, false\n   387\t}\n   388\t\n   389\tfunc (d AllocatableDevices) GetGPUDeviceByUUID(uuid string) *AllocatableDevice {\n   390\t\tfor _, device := range d {\n   391\t\t\tif device.Type() == Alongside the devices, it publishes\n    88\t   shared counters (one set per physical GPU) that track compute, memory, and\n    89\t   memory-slice usage. Each advertised partition declares how much of those\n    90\t   counters it would consume. This lets the scheduler allocate a partition that\n    91\t   does not yet exist, and prevents handing out two partitions that would\n    92\t   physically overlap on the same GPU.\n    93\t2. **Create the partition on demand.** After the scheduler allocates a partition\n    94\t   to a pod, the GPU kubelet plugin creates the actual MIG device when it\n    95\t   prepares the pod's resources on the node — enabling MIG mode on the GPU if\n    96\t   needed, then creating the GPU instance and compute instance for the requested\n    97\t   profile and placement.\n    98\t3. **Destroy the partition when it is no longer needed.** When the claim is\n    99\t   released, the driver tears the MIG device back down. The plugin uses a\n   100\t   node-local checkpoint as the source of truth, so it can reclaim partitions\n   101\t   left behind by a crash or partial preparation when it restarts.\n   102\t\n   103\tA request can be satisfied even when no matching partition was pre-configured —\n   104\tthe driver creates one to match.\n   105\t\n   106\tMIG slices support consumable capacity, time-slicing, and MPS sharing, using the same approaches as full GPUs.\n   107\tTime-slicing on MIG slices does not support interval configuration.\n   108\t\n   109\tTarget DeviceClass: `mig.nvidia.com`\n   110\t\n   111\tSee the [Request full GPUs guide](../guides/gpu-allocation/allocating-gpus.md) and\n   112\t[`demo/specs/quickstart/`](https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/dra-driver-nvidia-gpu/tree/{{< param driver_release_tag >}}/demo/specs/quickstart)\n   113\tfor GPU configuration examples. For MIG setup and workload examples, see the [MIG guide](../guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md).\n   114\t\n   115\t---\n   116\t\n   117\t### VFIO passthrough\n   118\t\n   119\tVFIO (Virtual Function I/O) passes a full physical GPU directly to a container, bypassing the NVIDIA driver stack in the host kernel. This gives the container raw hardware access and is intended for workloads that manage the GPU driver themselves, such as virtual machine managers or specialized research environments.\n   120\t\n--- site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:90\n    90\tDynamic MIG depends on the Kubernetes partitionable devices feature (KEP-4815).\n    91\tOn Kubernetes v1.34–v1.35, you must also enable the `DRAPartitionableDevices`\n    92\tfeature gate on the kube-apiserver and kube-scheduler. This feature gate is\n    93\trequired to allow the scheduler to allocate dynamically created MIG devices. On\n    94\tKubernetes v1.36 and later, `DRAPartitionableDevices` is enabled by default and\n    95\tno action is required.\n    96\t\n    97\t## Limitations and considerations\n    98\t\n    99\t- MIG is available only on NVIDIA data center GPUs with the Ampere architecture\n   100\t  or newer. Available profiles depend on the GPU model. For supported GPUs and\n   101\t  profiles, refer to the\n   102\t  [NVIDIA MIG User Guide](https://docs.nvidia.com/datacenter/tesla/mig-user-guide/#supported-gpus).\n   103\t- TimeSlicing has no effect on MIG devices. Setting `strategy: TimeSlicing` in a\n   104\t  `MigDeviceConfig` does not affect hardware behavior. To share a MIG slice\n   105\t  across containers, use Multi-Process Service (MPS) instead with the\n   106\t  `MPSSupport` feature gate. Note that `MPSSupport` is mutually exclusive with\n   107\t  `DynamicMIG`; they cannot be used together.\n   108\t- Static MIG partitions must be configured before the driver starts. In static\n   109\t  mode, the driver does not modify the node's MIG configuration. Partitions added\n   110\t  after the driver starts are not discovered until the GPU kubelet plugin\n   111\t  restarts.\n   112\t- Dynamic MIG replaces static discovery. On a MIG-capable node, the driver\n   113\t  manages partitions itself and does not use pre-created static partitions.\n   114\t  MIG partitions the driver did not create are destroyed when the GPU kubelet\n   115\t  plugin starts, so do not enable `DynamicMIG` on nodes with partitions you want\n   116\t  to keep.\n   117\t- On dynamic MIG nodes, let the driver\n   118\t  create and destroy partitions through workload requests. Do not run\n   119\t  `mig-parted` or `nvidia-smi mig` while the GPU kubelet plugin is running, because\n   120\t  manual changes can conflict with the driver's partition state and cause pod\n   121\t  preparation or cleanup to fail.\n   122\t- On Ampere GPUs (for example, A100), enable MIG mode on the GPU before the\n   123\t  DRA Driver starts. Ampere cannot toggle MIG mode without a GPU reset. If MIG\n   124\t  mode is off, the driver falls back to full-GPU allocation and advertises no\n   125\t  MIG partitions. Hopper and later architectures enable MIG mode on demand.\n   126\t- Dynamic MIG is not supported on vGPU guests.\n   127\t\n   128\t## Examples\n   129\t\n   130\tThe following examples work with static or dynamic MIG.\n   131\t\n   132\t### Create the example namespace\n   133\t\n   134\tThe examples use the `mig-example` namespace. Create it first:\n   135\t\n   136\t```bash\n   137\tkubectl create namespace mig-example\n   138\t```\n   139\t\n   140\t### Request any MIG device\n   141\t\n   142\tTo request any available MIG device without constraining the profile, use the\n   143\t`mig.nvidia.com` DeviceClass with no selectors.\n   144\t\n   145\t1. Create the `ResourceClaimTemplate`:\n   146\t\n   147\t   ```yaml\n   148\t   apiVersion: resource.k8s.io/v1\n   149\t   kind: ResourceClaimTemplate\n   150\t   metadata:\n   151\t     namespace: mig-example\n   152\t     name: any-mig\n   153\t   spec:\n   154\t     spec:\n   155\t       devices:\n   156\t         requests:\n   157\t         - name: mig\n   158\t           exactly:\n   159\t             deviceClassName: mig.nvidia.com\n   160\t   ```\n   161\t\n   162\t   Save it as `any-mig.yaml`.\n   163\t\n   164\t2. Apply the manifest:\n   165\t\n   166\t   ```bash\n   167\t   kubectl apply -f any-mig.yaml\n   168\t   ```\n   169\t\n   170\t3. Apply the manifest:\n   202\t\n   203\t   ```bash\n   204\t   kubectl apply -f mig-pod.yaml\n   205\t   ```\n   206\t\n   207\t5. {"type":"item.completed","item":{"id":"item_23","type":"agent_message","text":"**End-to-End Flow**\n\n- A dynamic MIG claim still targets the normal MIG `DeviceClass`, `mig.nvidia.com`; the difference from static MIG is not the claim shape, but that the driver advertises every possible partition up front, then creates the chosen one during kubelet prepare. See `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:76`.\n- Dynamic MIG requires the driver’s `DynamicMIG` gate, and on Kubernetes `v1.34`–`v1.35` also requires `DRAPartitionableDevices` on the apiserver and scheduler; from `v1.36` onward that Kubernetes gate is on by default. See `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:90`.\n- The request itself usually comes from a `ResourceClaimTemplate` or `ResourceClaim` that uses `mig.nvidia.com` and optionally a CEL selector over `device.attributes['gpu.nvidia.com']`; selectors use the fully qualified attribute domain, while capacities are read through `device.capacity['gpu.nvidia.com']`. See `site/content/docs/reference/resourceslice-attributes.md:312`.\n\n**1) DeviceClass Selection**\n\n- For a MIG request, the scheduler starts from the `mig.nvidia.com` `DeviceClass`, which conceptually selects devices whose `type` is MIG and then applies any claim selectors such as profile, memory, product name, or placement-related attributes. The MIG guide calls out that each MIG instance is exposed under `mig.nvidia.com`. See `site/content/docs/guides/gpu-allocation/mig.md:22`.\n- User config for the claim can include opaque driver config that resolves to `MigDeviceConfig`; the API package registers `MigDeviceConfig` as one of the supported config kinds, and notes that directly supplied config may be validated only in the `NodePrepareResources` path if it did not come through structured parameters. See `api/nvidia.com/resource/v1beta1/api.go:31` and `api/nvidia.com/resource/v1beta1/api.go:48`.\n\n**2) What the Scheduler Sees**\n\n- The kubelet plugin advertises dynamic MIG devices as canonical, not-yet-live partition candidates. Docs describe phase 1 as enumerating every valid profile/placement combination for each GPU and publishing shared counters so the scheduler can allocate a partition that does not yet exist without allowing overlapping placements. See `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:84`.\n- In code, allocatable devices are keyed by the canonical device name announced in the `ResourceSlice`; that exact name is later looked up during `NodePrepareResources`. The file explicitly says the device name is the canonical name announced in DRA and later resolved through the `AllocatableDevices` map. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/allocatable.go:30`.\n- Dynamic MIG allocatables are tracked separately from static MIG ones. `GetMigDynamicDevices()` enumerates them, and `GetMigDynamicDeviceByTuple()` resolves one by its abstract spec tuple rather than by a live MIG UUID. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/allocatable.go:180` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/allocatable.go:419`.\n- That tuple is the key abstraction for dynamic MIG: `MigDeviceInfo.SpecTuple()` is `(parent minor, profile ID, placement start)`, while `LiveTuple()` is only available once a real MIG device exists and includes GI/CI IDs and the MIG UUID. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/deviceinfo.go:132`.\n\n**3) Claim Allocation Outcome**\n\n- After scheduling, the claim’s allocation points at one of those canonical dynamic MIG device names on a chosen node. At this point Kubernetes has reserved an abstract partition candidate, not necessarily a preexisting MIG instance.\n- The skill’s requested trace boundary here is: `ResourceClaim` → scheduler binding → node assignment. The evidence at this stage is the claim’s allocation status plus the node’s `ResourceSlice` entry matching the canonical MIG device and its advertised counters; the actual MIG UUID does not exist yet for purely dynamic cases.\n\n**4) Kubelet Prepare Starts**\n\n- On the selected node, kubelet calls the GPU plugin’s prepare path. The central entry is `DeviceState.Prepare()`. It first validates admin access, takes the state lock, loads the checkpoint, and handles idempotency by checking whether the claim is already in `PrepareCompleted` or was only partially prepared earlier. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:291`.\n- Before doing the real device work, it stores the claim in the checkpoint with `ClaimCheckpointStatePrepareStarted`. That makes crash recovery and stale cleanup possible even if process death happens mid-prepare. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:348`.\n\n**5) Dynamic MIG Actualization**\n\n- For dynamic MIG, this is the transition from abstract scheduler allocation to concrete hardware state. The docs say phase 2 is: during prepare, the plugin enables MIG mode if needed, then creates the GPU instance and compute instance for the requested profile and placement. See `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:93`.\n- Concretely, the plugin starts from the canonical announced device name, resolves the corresponding dynamic allocatable from `AllocatableDevices`, and actualizes it into a live MIG instance. The important conceptual shift is from the `SpecTuple` announced to Kubernetes to the `LiveTuple` needed for device-node injection.\n- Once actualized, the device has GI/CI identifiers and a real MIG UUID, which are exactly what `LiveTuple()` carries. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/deviceinfo.go:140`.\n\n**6) CDI Spec Generation and Injection**\n\n- After preparing the device state, the plugin writes a transient per-claim CDI spec. `DeviceState.Prepare()` calls into CDI generation and then marks the claim as `PrepareCompleted` in the checkpoint. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:384` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:390`.\n- The CDI handler writes one transient spec per claim using a generated spec name based on claim UID. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:297`.\n- Each injected CDI device name is qualified as `k8s.gpu.nvidia.com/claim=<claimUID>-<canonicalDeviceName>`, built by `GetClaimDeviceName()`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:318`.\n- For a MIG device, CDI must expose:\n  - the parent `/dev/nvidia<Pm>` node,\n  - the GI capability node,\n  - the CI capability node.\n  The MIG-specific helper builds the latter two from `/proc/driver/nvidia-caps/...` using the live GI/CI identifiers. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:327` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:344`.\n- That CDI spec is what the container runtime consumes. The result is that the workload sees the correct live MIG device, even though scheduling happened against an abstract pre-advertised partition.\n\n**7) Checkpoint Role During Runtime**\n\n- The checkpoint is the node-local source of truth for prepared claims. It records claim UID, checkpoint state, and claim status; on startup the plugin either reuses a valid checkpoint for the current boot or creates a fresh one. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:243` and `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/device_state.go:282`.\n- This matters especially for dynamic MIG because hardware can be left half-configured if the plugin crashes after creating the MIG instance but before finishing CDI setup. The docs explicitly call out checkpoint-based reclamation for crash/partial-prepare recovery. See `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:98`.\n\n**8) Teardown / Unprepare**\n\n- When the claim is released, kubelet calls unprepare. The docs describe phase 3 as destroying the partition when no longer needed. See `site/content/docs/concepts/gpu-allocation.md:98`.\n- In practice, unprepare uses the checkpoint to find what this node previously prepared, reverses driver-owned state, and deletes the per-claim CDI spec. The CDI cleanup hook is `DeleteClaimSpecFile()`. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:308`.\n- If a claim was only in `PrepareStarted`, cleanup logic can still detect it as stale and attempt reclamation later. The cleanup manager scans checkpoint entries in `PrepareStarted` state and calls `unprepareIfStale()` based on API-server truth, specifically to recover from interrupted prepares. See `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cleanup.go:93`.\n- Dynamic MIG is therefore symmetric:\n  - scheduler allocates an abstract candidate,\n  - prepare creates the real MIG partition,\n  - CDI exposes it to the container,\n  - unprepare removes the CDI spec and tears the MIG partition back down when the claim is gone.\n\n**Evidence at Each Boundary**\n\n- **Claim intent:** `ResourceClaim` / `ResourceClaimTemplate` and any selector expressions.\n- **Class and selectors:** `mig.nvidia.com` plus CEL over `device.attributes['gpu.nvidia.com']`; see `site/content/docs/reference/resourceslice-attributes.md:318`.\n- **Scheduler allocation:** claim status allocation and node `ResourceSlice` entry for the canonical dynamic MIG device.\n- **Prepare success:** kubelet event stream and plugin logs around `PrepareStarted`/`PrepareCompleted`; checkpoint state in the plugin checkpoint.\n- **CDI injection:** transient CDI YAML under the configured CDI root and returned qualified CDI device names; see `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/cdi.go:300`.\n- **Runtime visibility:** container sees the injected NVIDIA device nodes and capabilities.\n- **Teardown:** CDI spec removal plus disappearance of the live MIG partition after claim release.\n\n**Key Distinction**\n\n- The core dynamic MIG trick is that **the scheduler binds an abstract partition spec, while kubelet prepare turns that spec into a live MIG instance and then injects it through CDI**. The abstraction boundary is encoded directly in `SpecTuple()` versus `LiveTuple()` in `cmd/gpu-kubelet-plugin/deviceinfo.go:132`.\n\nIf you want, I can turn this into a shorter operator-facing checklist or a sequence diagram next."}}
